You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Walter and Jesse's friend Mike had helped them in making Crymeth and hence, they wanted to give him a share.
For deciding the share, they both decided to choose one number each, X and Y and found out that K^th Highest Common Factor of their two numbers is a good amount of Crymeth that can be given to Mike .
Walter and Jesse did this activity for D days in total. For each day, find out what quantity of Crymeth they decided to give to Mike.

Input:

First line contains a natural number D - the total number of days.
D lines follow. Each line contains three natural numbers - X, Y and K.

Output:

For each day, print the quantity given to Mike on a new line.
If the K^th HCF of X and Y does not exist, simply print "No crymeth today" (Without the quotes)

Constraints:
1 ≤ D ≤ 10^3
1 ≤ X, Y, K ≤ 10^10

SAMPLE INPUT
3
8 16 2
8 16 4
8 16 5

SAMPLE OUTPUT
4
1
No crymeth today

Explanation

1st Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 8
2nd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 4
3rd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 2
4th Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 1

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to find the K-th highest common factor among all common factors

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **Find GCD of X and Y**: All common factors of X and Y are the factors of GCD(X, Y)

2. **Find all factors of GCD**: The common factors are exactly the divisors of GCD(X, Y)

3. **Sort factors in descending order**: To find the K-th highest common factor

4. **Return the K-th factor**: If K is valid (within the range of factors), return it. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each test case (X, Y, K):
   - Calculate GCD(X, Y)
   - Find all divisors of GCD(X, Y)
   - Sort divisors in descending order
   - If K is within the valid range (1 to number of divisors), return the K-th divisor
   - Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

## Implementation Considerations

- GCD calculation: Use Euclidean algorithm
- Finding divisors: We only need to check up to sqrt(GCD)
- For efficiency: Since we want the K-th highest, we can find divisors and sort them in descending order

## Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 0:
        return []
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    # Check divisors up to sqrt(n)
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            # Add the corresponding divisor (n/i) if different from i
            if i != n // i:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Calculate GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if k-th highest exists
    if k <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[k - 1]  # k-1 because we want 1-indexed position
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read number of days
d = int(input())

# Process each day
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

For input "8 16 2":
- GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [1, 2, 4, 8] → sorted descending: [8, 4, 2, 1]
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

For input "8 16 4":
- GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [1, 2, 4, 8] → sorted descending: [8, 4, 2, 1]
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

For input "8 16 5":
- GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Only 4 divisors exist, so 5th doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution matches the expected output!

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th Highest Common Factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We need to find the K-th largest common factor of X and Y
4. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (i.e., K is larger than the number of common factors), we output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Understanding Common Factors
- The common factors of two numbers are the divisors that divide both numbers
- All common factors of X and Y are the divisors of their GCD
- If GCD(X,Y) = G, then all common factors are the divisors of G

### Step 2: Finding the K-th Highest Common Factor
- We need to find all divisors of GCD(X,Y)
- Sort them in descending order
- Return the K-th one if it exists
- If K is greater than the total number of divisors, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Algorithm Steps
1. For each day, calculate GCD(X,Y)
2. Find all divisors of the GCD
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. Check if K is within valid range (1 to number of divisors)
5. Return the K-th divisor or "No crymeth today"

### Step 4: Optimization
- We don't need to find all divisors explicitly; we can find them by iterating up to √GCD
- For each divisor d found, we also get GCD/d as a divisor
- We store all divisors and sort them

### Step 5: Implementation

```python
import math

def get_divisors(n):
    """Return all divisors of n in ascending order"""
    divisors = []
    for i in range(1, int(math.sqrt(n)) + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    return sorted(divisors)

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = math.gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Sort in descending order for highest to lowest
    divisors.sort(reverse=True)
    
    # Check if K-th divisor exists
    if k <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[k - 1]  # k-th highest (k-1 index)
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input and solve
d = int(input())
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

Given: X=8, Y=16, K=2

1. GCD(8,16) = 8
2. Divisors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
3. Sorted in descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1
4. 2nd highest = 4 ✓

Given: X=8, Y=16, K=4

1. GCD(8,16) = 8
2. Divisors: 8, 4, 2, 1
3. 4th highest = 1 ✓

Given: X=8, Y=16, K=5

1. GCD(8,16) = 8
2. Only 4 divisors exist
3. K=5 > 4, so "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution matches the expected output.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th Highest Common Factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We need to find the K-th largest common divisor of X and Y
4. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (K is larger than the total number of common divisors), output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-step Analysis

1. **Common divisors of two numbers**: The common divisors of X and Y are exactly the divisors of GCD(X, Y)

2. **Finding common divisors**: 
   - First find GCD(X, Y)
   - Then find all divisors of this GCD
   - Sort them in descending order
   - Return the K-th one (if it exists)

3. **Key insight**: The common divisors of X and Y are exactly the divisors of GCD(X, Y)

4. **Edge cases**:
   - If K is larger than the total number of divisors of GCD(X, Y), return "No crymeth today"
   - If GCD(X, Y) is 1, then it has only one divisor (1)

## Algorithm Steps

1. For each day, read X, Y, K
2. Calculate GCD(X, Y)
3. Find all divisors of GCD(X, Y)
4. Sort divisors in descending order
5. If K is within valid range (1 to number of divisors), return the K-th divisor
6. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

## Implementation Considerations

- Finding divisors efficiently: We only need to iterate up to √(GCD) to find all divisors
- For each divisor i found, we also get GCD//i as a divisor
- We need to be careful with perfect squares (i = √GCD)

```python
import math
from math import gcd

def get_divisors(n):
    """Returns list of all divisors of n in ascending order"""
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors)

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if k-th HCF exists
    if k > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    # Return k-th highest common factor (k-th in descending order)
    # Since divisors are in ascending order, we need to reverse it
    return divisors[-k]

# Read number of days
d = int(input())

# Process each day
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the ordering. The problem asks for the K-th highest common factor, which means:

- 1st highest = largest common factor
- 2nd highest = second largest common factor
- etc.

So if I have divisors in ascending order, I need the K-th from the end (i.e., index -k in 0-indexed).

Let me fix this:

```python
import math
from math import gcd

def get_divisors(n):
    """Returns list of all divisors of n in ascending order"""
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors)

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if k-th HCF exists
    if k > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    # The k-th highest is the k-th from the end (in descending order)
    # Since we have divisors in ascending order, we want divisors[-k]
    return divisors[-k]

# Read number of days
d = int(input())

# Process each day
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the Kth highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
4. We need to find the Kth highest common factor (i.e., the Kth largest divisor of GCD(X,Y))

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
- The common factors of X and Y are exactly the divisors of GCD(X,Y)

### Step 2: Find all divisors of GCD(X,Y)
- The divisors of a number are all numbers that divide it evenly
- We need to find the Kth largest divisor

### Step 3: Handle edge cases
- If K is greater than the total number of divisors, output "No crymeth today"
- If K is 1, we return the largest divisor (which is GCD(X,Y) itself)

### Step 4: Algorithm for finding divisors
- For a number N, we can find all divisors by checking from 1 to √N
- For each divisor i found, we also get N/i as a divisor
- This gives us all divisors efficiently

### Step 5: Sort divisors in descending order
- Since we want the Kth highest, we sort divisors in descending order
- Then pick the Kth element (index K-1)

## Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(X, Y, K):
    # Find GCD of X and Y
    g = gcd(X, Y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if Kth highest exists
    if K > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    return divisors[K - 1]

# Read input and solve
D = int(input())
for _ in range(D):
    X, Y, K = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(X, Y, K)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample Input

For X=8, Y=16, K=2:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [8, 4, 2, 1] (in descending order)
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=4:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: [8, 4, 2, 1]
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

For X=8, Y=16, K=5:
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Only 4 divisors exist, K=5 is out of bounds
- "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution is correct and handles all edge cases.

    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    if n == 1:
        return [1]
    
    divisors = []
    sqrt_n = int(math.sqrt(n))
    
    for i in range(1, sqrt_n + 1):
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
    
    return sorted(divisors, reverse=True)

def solve(X, Y, K):
    # Find GCD of X and Y
    g = gcd(X, Y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD in descending order
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Check if Kth highest exists
    if K > len(divisors):
        return "No crymeth today"
    
    return divisors[K - 1]

# Read input and solve
D = int(input())
for _ in range(D):
    X, Y, K = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(X, Y, K)
    print(result)

For deciding the share, they both decided to choose one number each, X and Y and found out that K^th Highest Common Factor of their two numbers is a good amount of Crymeth that can be given to Mike .
Walter and Jesse did this activity for D days in total. For each day, find out what quantity of Crymeth they decided to give to Mike.

Input:

First line contains a natural number D - the total number of days.
D lines follow. Each line contains three natural numbers - X, Y and K.

Output:

For each day, print the quantity given to Mike on a new line.
If the K^th HCF of X and Y does not exist, simply print "No crymeth today" (Without the quotes)

Constraints:
1 ≤ D ≤ 10^3
1 ≤ X, Y, K ≤ 10^10

SAMPLE INPUT
3
8 16 2
8 16 4
8 16 5

SAMPLE OUTPUT
4
1
No crymeth today

Explanation

1st Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 8
2nd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 4
3rd Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 2
4th Highest Common Factor of 8 and 16 = 1

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th Highest Common Factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We need to find the K-th largest common factor of X and Y
4. If the K-th HCF doesn't exist (i.e., K is larger than the total number of common factors), print "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Understanding Common Factors
- The common factors of two numbers are the factors of their GCD
- For example, GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Factors of 8 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- So the common factors of 8 and 16 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- Sorted in descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1

### Step 2: Algorithm
1. Find GCD of X and Y
2. Find all factors of the GCD
3. Sort factors in descending order
4. If K is within the range (1 to number of factors), return the K-th factor
5. Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Implementation Challenges
- X, Y, K can be up to 10^10, so we can't generate all factors of GCD directly if GCD is very large
- However, we only need up to K factors, and K can be large but we have a bound
- We need an efficient way to generate factors in descending order

### Step 4: Optimized Approach
- Instead of generating all factors, we can iterate through potential divisors
- For each divisor d that divides GCD, it's a common factor
- We can generate factors in descending order by checking divisors from sqrt(GCD) down

## Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_factors_gcd(gcd_val):
    """Get all factors of gcd_val in descending order"""
    factors = []
    # Find all divisors from 1 to sqrt(gcd_val)
    i = 1
    while i * i <= gcd_val:
        if gcd_val % i == 0:
            factors.append(i)
            # Add the corresponding factor (gcd_val // i) if it's different
            if i != gcd_val // i:
                factors.append(gcd_val // i)
        i += 1
    
    # Sort in descending order
    factors.sort(reverse=True)
    return factors

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # If GCD is 1, only factor is 1
    if g == 1:
        factors = [1]
    else:
        factors = get_factors_gcd(g)
    
    # Check if k-th factor exists
    if k <= len(factors):
        return factors[k - 1]  # k-th factor (1-indexed)
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input
d = int(input())
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

## Verification with Sample

For input `8 16 2`:
- GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Factors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8 → sorted desc: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

For input `8 16 4`:
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

For input `8 16 5`:
- Only 4 factors exist, so 5th doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution handles the constraints properly and efficiently finds the K-th highest common factor.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We need to find the K-th largest common factor of X and Y
4. If such a factor doesn't exist, print "No crymeth today"

## Key Insights

1. The common factors of two numbers are the divisors of their GCD
2. If we find GCD(X, Y), then all common factors are the divisors of this GCD
3. We need to find the K-th largest divisor of GCD(X, Y)
4. If K is greater than the total number of divisors of GCD(X, Y), then the K-th HCF doesn't exist

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Find GCD of X and Y
- Use Euclidean algorithm to find GCD(X, Y)

### Step 2: Find all divisors of GCD
- Find all divisors of the GCD value
- Sort them in descending order to get the highest to lowest

### Step 3: Check if K-th HCF exists
- If K is within the range of divisors (1 to total_divisors), return the K-th one
- Otherwise, return "No crymeth today"

### Step 4: Implementation considerations
- Since X, Y can be up to 10^10, we need to be efficient in finding divisors
- We only need the K-th largest divisor, not all of them
- We can find divisors by iterating from 1 to √GCD

## Key Optimization
Instead of finding all divisors, we can:
1. Find GCD(X, Y)
2. Find divisors of GCD up to √GCD
3. For each divisor d, we get d and GCD/d as divisors
4. Sort all divisors in descending order
5. Return the K-th one if it exists

## Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    # Only iterate up to sqrt(n)
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            # Add the corresponding divisor (n/i) if it's different
            if i != n // i:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

def solve():
    d = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(d):
        x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find GCD of x and y
        g = gcd(x, y)
        
        # Get all divisors of g
        divisors = get_divisors(g)
        
        # Sort in descending order to get highest first
        divisors.sort(reverse=True)
        
        # Check if k-th HCF exists
        if k <= len(divisors):
            print(divisors[k-1])  # k-1 because we use 0-based indexing
        else:
            print("No crymeth today")

solve()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. The common factors of two numbers are the numbers that divide both numbers
4. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (in descending order)

## Step-by-Step Analysis

### Step 1: Understanding Common Factors
For two numbers X and Y, their common factors are the divisors of GCD(X, Y).

For example:
- X = 8, Y = 16
- GCD(8, 16) = 8
- Divisors of 8 are: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1

### Step 2: Finding the K-th Highest Common Factor
1. Find GCD(X, Y)
2. Find all divisors of GCD(X, Y)
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. If K is within the range of divisors, return the K-th one, otherwise return "No crymeth today"

### Step 3: Computing Divisors Efficiently
Instead of checking all numbers up to GCD(X, Y), we can find divisors by checking only up to √GCD(X, Y).

For each divisor i found, we also get GCD(X, Y) // i as a divisor (unless i is the square root).

### Step 4: Implementation Plan
1. For each test case, compute GCD(X, Y)
2. Generate all divisors of GCD(X, Y)
3. Sort divisors in descending order
4. If K ≤ number of divisors, return the K-th divisor; otherwise return "No crymeth today"

## Edge Cases
- If K is larger than the number of divisors, we return "No crymeth today"
- If K is 1, we return the largest divisor (which is the GCD itself)

```python
import math
from math import gcd

def get_divisors(n):
    """Return all divisors of n in ascending order"""
    divisors = []
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return sorted(divisors)

def solve():
    d = int(input())
    
    for _ in range(d):
        x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find GCD of x and y
        g = gcd(x, y)
        
        # Get all divisors of GCD
        divisors = get_divisors(g)
        
        # Sort in descending order (highest first)
        divisors.sort(reverse=True)
        
        # Check if k-th highest exists
        if k <= len(divisors):
            print(divisors[k-1])  # k-1 because of 0-indexing
        else:
            print("No crymeth today")

solve()
```

## Verification with Sample Input
- Day 1: X=8, Y=16, K=2
  - GCD(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors of 8: [1,2,4,8] → sorted descending: [8,4,2,1]
  - 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- Day 2: X=8, Y=16, K=4
  - GCD(8,16) = 8
  - Divisors: [8,4,2,1]
  - 4th highest = 1 ✓

- Day 3: X=8, Y=16, K=5
  - We only have 4 divisors, K=5 is too large
  - "No crymeth today" ✓

The solution correctly handles all test cases.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the K-th highest common factor (HCF) of two numbers X and Y
2. HCF means Highest Common Factor, which is the same as GCD (Greatest Common Divisor)
3. We want the K-th highest common factor, meaning we list all common factors in descending order and pick the K-th one
4. If the K-th factor doesn't exist (K is larger than the total number of common factors), we output "No crymeth today"

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Understand what common factors are
- Common factors of X and Y are the numbers that divide both X and Y
- These are exactly the divisors of GCD(X, Y)

### Step 2: Find all common factors
- First, find GCD(X, Y)
- Then find all divisors of this GCD
- These divisors are the common factors in descending order

### Step 3: Find the K-th highest common factor
- Sort divisors in descending order
- Return the K-th element if it exists, otherwise "No crymeth today"

### Step 4: Implementation considerations
- Since X, Y can be up to 10^10, we need an efficient way to find divisors
- We only need to check divisors up to √(GCD(X,Y))
- For each divisor i found, we also get GCD/i as a divisor
- We collect all divisors and sort them in descending order

Let me trace through the example:
- X=8, Y=16, K=2
- GCD(8,16) = 8
- Divisors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8
- In descending order: 8, 4, 2, 1
- 2nd highest = 4 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=4  
- 4th highest = 1 ✓

- X=8, Y=16, K=5
- Only 4 common factors, so K=5 doesn't exist → "No crymeth today" ✓

## Code Implementation

```python
import math

def gcd(a, b):
    while b:
        a, b = b, a % b
    return a

def get_divisors(n):
    divisors = []
    # Find all divisors up to sqrt(n)
    i = 1
    while i * i <= n:
        if n % i == 0:
            divisors.append(i)
            if i != n // i:  # Avoid adding the same divisor twice for perfect squares
                divisors.append(n // i)
        i += 1
    return divisors

def solve(x, y, k):
    # Find GCD of x and y
    g = gcd(x, y)
    
    # Get all divisors of GCD
    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Sort in descending order
    divisors.sort(reverse=True)
    
    # Check if k-th highest exists
    if k <= len(divisors):
        return divisors[k - 1]  # k-1 because indexing starts at 0
    else:
        return "No crymeth today"

# Read input and solve
d = int(input())
for _ in range(d):
    x, y, k = map(int, input().split())
    result = solve(x, y, k)
    print(result)
```

The solution works as follows:

1. **GCD calculation**: Use Euclidean algorithm to find GCD of X and Y efficiently
2. **Divisor collection**: Find all divisors of GCD by checking numbers from 1 to √GCD
3. **Sorting**: Sort divisors in descending order to get highest to lowest
4. **K-th factor**: Return the K-th factor if it exists, otherwise "No crymeth today"

Time complexity: O(√GCD) for each query, which is efficient given the constraints.

        i += 1
    return divisors

    g = gcd(x, y)

    divisors = get_divisors(g)
    
    # Sort in descending order
    divisors.sort(reverse=True)
    
    # Check if k-th highest exists

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

During a break in the buffet of the scientific lyceum of the Kingdom of Kremland, there was formed a queue of $n$ high school students numbered from $1$ to $n$. Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Dissatisfaction of the person $i$ equals the product of $a_i$ by the number of people standing to the left of his position, add the product $b_i$ by the number of people standing to the right of his position. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 2
2 3
6 1

Output
12
Input
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

Output
25
Input
10
5 10
12 4
31 45
20 55
30 17
29 30
41 32
7 1
5 5
3 15

Output
1423


-----Note-----

In the first example it is optimal to put people in this order: ($3, 1, 2$). The first person is in the position of $2$, then his dissatisfaction will be equal to $4 \cdot 1+2 \cdot 1=6$. The second person is in the position of $3$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $2 \cdot 2+3 \cdot 0=4$. The third person is in the position of $1$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $6 \cdot 0+1 \cdot 2=2$. The total dissatisfaction will be $12$.

In the second example, you need to put people in this order: ($3, 2, 4, 1$). The total dissatisfaction will be $25$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`. When student `i` is at position `j` (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is:
- `a_i * (j-1)` + `b_i * (n-j)`

We need to arrange all students to minimize the total dissatisfaction.

## Key Insight

Let me rewrite the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- `= (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for student `i` at position `j`, dissatisfaction = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

## Strategy

The key insight is that we want to assign students to positions based on their `(a_i - b_i)` value.

- If `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, the student "prefers" to be at the end (higher position number) because the coefficient of `j` is negative
- If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, the student "prefers" to be at the start (lower position number) because the coefficient of `j` is positive

Actually, let's think about this differently. We want to minimize the sum of `(a_i - b_i) * j` across all students.

The sum of all `j` from 1 to n is `n(n+1)/2`, but we need to assign each student to a unique position.

The optimal strategy is to sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order and place them in positions 1 to n respectively.

Why? Because:
- Students with more negative `(a_i - b_i)` should go to the front (smaller j)
- Students with more positive `(a_i - b_i)` should go to the back (larger j)

This minimizes the sum of `(a_i - b_i) * j` since we're pairing negative coefficients with small j values and positive coefficients with large j values.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input
2. For each student, calculate `diff_i = a_i - b_i`
3. Sort students by `diff_i` in ascending order
4. Place the sorted students in positions 1, 2, ..., n
5. Calculate total dissatisfaction

- Student 1: a=4, b=2, diff=2
- Student 2: a=2, b=3, diff=-1  
- Student 3: a=6, b=1, diff=5

Sorted by diff: student 2 (diff=-1), student 1 (diff=2), student 3 (diff=5)
Positions: 1, 2, 3

Dissatisfaction:
- Student 2 at pos 1: 2*(-1)*1 + (3*3 - 2*(-1)) = -2 + (9 + 2) = 9? 

I think I made a calculation error. Let me recalculate properly.

Actually, let me go back to the original formula:
For student i at position j, dissatisfaction = `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`

Total dissatisfaction = Σ[a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)] for all students i at position j.

This equals: Σ[a_i * (j-1)] + Σ[b_i * (n-j)]

We can rearrange this as: Σ[a_i * (j-1)] + Σ[b_i * (n-j)]

The total dissatisfaction is minimized when we sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order.

Let me implement this:

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b, i))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1])

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for pos in range(n):
    a, b, original_idx = students[pos]
    # Student is at position pos+1 (1-indexed)
    j = pos + 1
    dissatisfaction = a * (j - 1) + b * (n - j)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

```python
n = int(input())
```

## Problem Understanding

We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`. When student `i` is at position `j` (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is:
- `a_i * (j-1)` for the number of people to their left
- `b_i * (n-j)` for the number of people to their right

- = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So the algorithm is:
1. For each student, calculate c_i = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by c_i in descending order
3. Assign position j to the student at index j-1 (1-indexed)
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction = Σ(c_i * j) + (n * Σb_i - Σa_i)

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
total_b = 0
total_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a - b, a, b))
    total_b += b
    total_a += a

# Sort by (a - b) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Calculate the sum of (a_i - b_i) * j_i
cost_sum = 0
for j in range(n):
    c, a, b = students[j]
    cost_sum += c * (j + 1)  # j+1 is the position (1-indexed)

# Constant term: n * sum(b_i) - sum(a_i)
constant_term = n * total_b - total_a

total_dissatisfaction = cost_sum + constant_term
print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

constant_term = 4*11 - 14 = 44 - 14 = 30

Total dissatisfaction = -4 + 30 = 26

But expected is 25. Hmm, off by 1.

Let me recalculate manually.

Students after sorting by (a-b) descending:
- Student 3: a=7, b=1, at position 1: diss = 7*0 + 1*(4-1) = 0 + 3 = 3
- Student 2: a=3, b=3, at position 2: diss = 3*1 + 3*1 = 3 + 3 = 6
- Student 1: a=2, b=4, at position 3: diss = 2*2 + 4*0 = 4 + 0 = 4
- Student 4: a=2, b=3, at position 4: diss = 2*3 + 3*0 = 6 + 0 = 6
Total = 3 + 6 + 4 + 6 = 19

This doesn't match either.

I think I made an error in the coupling between sorting and the formula.

Let me go back to the original formula and double-check my derivation.

Original dissatisfaction for student i at position j:
a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)

I rewrite it as:
a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)
= a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j
= (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)

Yes, this is correct.

Now, the total for all students:
Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i)]
= Σ(a_i - b_i) * j_i + Σ(b_i * n - a_i)

The second term is indeed constant: n * Σb_i - Σa_i

For the first term, we want to minimize Σ(c_i * j_i) where c_i = a_i - b_i.

To minimize this, since it's a sum of c_i * j_i, we should:
- For large positive c_i, assign small j_i (small position)
- For large negative c_i, assign large j_i (large position)

This means we should sort by c_i in descending order and assign positions 1,2,3,... 

But my calculation gives 26, expected is 25.

Let me try sorting by c_i in ascending order:
c values: -2, -1, 0, 6

Positions: 1,2,3,4
c * j: (-2)*1 + (-1)*2 + 0*3 + 6*4 = -2 -2 + 0 + 24 = 20
Total dissatisfaction = 20 + (4*11 - 14) = 20 + 30 = 50

Not 25.

I need to manually calculate what order gives 25.

The example says order is (3,2,4,1), which means:
- Position 1: student 3 (a=7,b=1)
- Position 2: student 2 (a=3,b=3)  
- Position 3: student 4 (a=2,b=3)
- Position 4: student 1 (a=2,b=4)

Dissatisfactions:
1: 7*0 + 1*3 = 0 + 3 = 3
2: 3*1 + 3*1 = 3 + 3 = 6
3: 2*2 + 3*0 = 4 + 0 = 4
4: 2*3 + 4*0 = 6 + 0 = 6
Total = 3+6+4+6 = 19

That's not 25.

Let me re-read the input.

Input:
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

So student 1: (2,4)
student 2: (3,3)
student 3: (7,1)
student 4: (2,3)

Order (3,2,4,1) means:
- position 1: student 3: a=7,b=1 → 7*0 + 1*(4-1) = 0 + 3 = 3
- position 2: student 2: a=3,b=3 → 3*1 + 3*(4-2) = 3 + 6 = 9
- position 3: student 4: a=2,b=3 → 2*2 + 3*1 = 4 + 3 = 7
- position 4: student 1: a=2,b=4 → 2*3 + 4*0 = 6 + 0 = 6
Total = 3+9+7+6 = 25

Yes! So my manual calculation for position 2 was wrong.

For position 2: people to the left = 1, people to the right = 4-2 = 2
So diss = a*1 + b*2 = 3*1 + 3*2 = 3+6=9

So the formula is correct.

Now let's use my derivation to find the optimal assignment.

With this arrangement (3,2,4,1):
c_i values:
3: 7-1 = 6
2: 3-3 = 0
4: 2-3 = -1
1: 2-4 = -2

Positions: 1,2,3,4
c_i * j_i: 6*1 + 0*2 + (-1)*3 + (-2)*4 = 6 + 0 -3 -8 = -5

Constant term: 4*(4+3+1+3) - (2+3+7+2) = 4*11 - 14 = 44-14 = 30

Total = -5 + 30 = 25

Perfect! So the sum of c_i * j_i is -5.

Now, which sorting gives this?

If I sort by c_i descending: 6, 0, -1, -2 → positions 1,2,3,4 → 6*1 + 0*2 + (-1)*3 + (-2)*4 = 6 -3 -8 = -5

Exactly what we have!

So sorting by c_i in descending order gives the optimal result.

The algorithm is correct.

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
total_b = 0
total_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a - b, a, b))
    total_b += b
    total_a += a

# Sort by (a - b) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Calculate the sum of (a_i - b_i) * j_i
cost_sum = 0
for j in range(n):
    c, a, b = students[j]
    cost_sum += c * (j + 1)  # j+1 is the position (1-indexed)

# Constant term: n * sum(b_i) - sum(a_i)
constant_term = n * total_b - total_a

total_dissatisfaction = cost_sum + constant_term
print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

During a break in the buffet of the scientific lyceum of the Kingdom of Kremland, there was formed a queue of $n$ high school students numbered from $1$ to $n$. Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`. When student `i` is at position `j` (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is:
- `a_i * (j-1)` for the people to their left
- `b_i * (n-j)` for the people to their right

Total dissatisfaction is the sum of all individual dissatisfactions.

## Key Insight

Let's think about what happens when we rearrange students. For any student `i` placed at position `j`, their contribution is `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`.

We can rewrite this as: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j = (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So the total dissatisfaction becomes:
`Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i)]` for all students i at position j_i

This can be split into:
`Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i] + Σ(b_i * n - a_i)`

The second term `Σ(b_i * n - a_i)` is constant regardless of arrangement (since it doesn't depend on positions). 

The first term `Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i]` depends on how we assign positions to students.

## Optimization Strategy

To minimize the total dissatisfaction, we want to minimize `Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i]`.

This is a classic "greedy" problem. We should assign students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` values to the left positions (smaller j) and students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` values to the right positions (larger j).

Why? Because:
- If `(a_i - b_i)` is negative, we want to put student i at a small position j to minimize the negative contribution
- If `(a_i - b_i)` is positive, we want to put student i at a large position j to minimize the positive contribution

Actually, let me reconsider: we want to minimize `(a_i - b_i) * j_i`. 

For negative `(a_i - b_i)` values, putting them at small j makes the product less negative (more positive), which is good. 
For positive `(a_i - b_i)` values, putting them at large j makes the product more positive, which is bad.

Wait, I need to think more carefully.

We want to minimize `Σ(c_i * j_i)` where `c_i = a_i - b_i`.

To minimize this sum, we should:
- Assign students with negative `c_i` to small positions (since negative * small = more negative, which is better)
- Assign students with positive `c_i` to large positions (since positive * large = large positive, but we want to minimize total)

Actually, the key insight is that to minimize `Σ(c_i * j_i)`, we should sort students by `c_i = a_i - b_i` and assign the smallest positions to those with the smallest `c_i`.

More precisely: sort students by `a_i - b_i` in ascending order, then assign position 1 to the student with the smallest `a_i - b_i`, position 2 to the next, etc.

This is because if we have two students i and j where `c_i < c_j`, and we swap their positions, we want to see if it helps.

But actually, this is a well-known greedy result: to minimize `Σ(c_i * j_i)` with fixed values, we should sort by `c_i` and assign in ascending order of `c_i`.

Wait, let me verify with a simple example.

If `c_i < c_j`, and we put student i at position j and student j at position i, then the change in total is:
`(c_i * j - c_i * i) + (c_j * i - c_j * j) = c_i * (j-i) + c_j * (i-j) = (c_i - c_j) * (j-i)`

Since `c_i < c_j`, `c_i - c_j < 0`, and if `j > i`, then `(j-i) > 0`, so the total changes by a negative value (better). 

So if we have `c_i < c_j` and `i > j`, swapping them improves the result.

Therefore, we should sort by `c_i` in ascending order and assign positions in order.

Actually, let me reconsider the calculation:

If we have student i with `c_i` and student j with `c_j`, and we swap them:
- Original: `c_i * pos_i + c_j * pos_j`
- After swap: `c_i * pos_j + c_j * pos_i`
- Difference: `(c_i * pos_j + c_j * pos_i) - (c_i * pos_i + c_j * pos_j) = c_i * (pos_j - pos_i) + c_j * (pos_i - pos_j) = (c_i - c_j) * (pos_j - pos_i)`

For this to be negative (improvement), we need `(c_i - c_j) * (pos_j - pos_i) < 0`.

If `c_i < c_j`, then `c_i - c_j < 0`, so we need `pos_j - pos_i > 0` (i.e., `pos_j > pos_i`).

So if `c_i < c_j` and `pos_j > pos_i`, then swapping improves the result.

This means we want to assign students with smaller `c_i` to larger positions? Wait, no.

We need `(c_i - c_j) * (pos_j - pos_i) < 0` for improvement.

If `c_i < c_j`, then we need `pos_j - pos_i > 0` for improvement.

This means we want to put the student with smaller `c_i` at a larger position to improve.

But that seems counterintuitive.

Actually, let me recheck: if `c_i < c_j` and we have `pos_j > pos_i`, then `pos_j - pos_i > 0`, so `(c_i - c_j) * (pos_j - pos_i) < 0`, so the swap improves the total. 

So we want to put students with smaller `c_i` at larger positions.

But this means we should sort by `c_i` in descending order and assign positions in ascending order.

Let me verify with the first example:
Student 1: a=4,b=2, c=4-2=2
Student 2: a=2,b=3, c=2-3=-1  
Student 3: a=6,b=1, c=6-1=5

c values: 2, -1, 5
Sort by c in descending order: 5, 2, -1

Assign positions: 1,2,3
- Student 3 at pos 1: c=5 * 1 = 5
- Student 1 at pos 2: c=2 * 2 = 4  
- Student 2 at pos 3: c=-1 * 3 = -3
Total = 5+4-3 = 6

The constant term: Σ(b_i * n - a_i) = (2*3-4) + (3*3-2) + (1*3-6) = (6-4)+(9-2)+(3-6) = 2+7-3 = 6

Total dissatisfaction = 6 + 6 = 12. This matches the expected answer!

So the algorithm is:
1. Calculate c_i = a_i - b_i for each student
2. Sort students by c_i in descending order (largest c_i first)
3. Assign the student with largest c_i to position 1, next to position 2, etc.
4. Calculate the positional contribution: Σ(c_i * j_i) for j_i being position (1-indexed)
5. Add the constant term Σ(b_i * n - a_i)

Actually, I think I have it backwards. Let me recheck the swap analysis.

If we swap two students where `c_i < c_j` and initially `pos_i < pos_j`, then the difference is `(c_i - c_j) * (pos_j - pos_i) < 0` since `c_i - c_j < 0` and `pos_j - pos_i > 0`. So the difference is negative, meaning the swap improves the sum. 

This means we want to swap so that students with smaller c_i end up at larger positions.

In other words, we want to assign positions in descending order of c_i.

So students with larger c_i get smaller positions, students with smaller c_i get larger positions.

Sort by c_i in descending order and assign positions 1, 2, 3, ...

But let me verify with the swap: if we have c_i < c_j, and we put student i at a larger position than student j, then we have improved.

Yes, that makes sense.

So algorithm:
1. For each student, calculate c_i = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by c_i in descending order
3. For the i-th student in this sorted order (0-indexed), they are at position (i+1)
4. Their contribution to the variable part is c_i * (i+1)
5. The constant part is sum over all students of (b_i * n - a_i)
6. Total = sum of variable part + constant part

```python
n = int(input())
students = []

for _ in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Calculate c_i = a_i - b_i for each student
c_values = []
for a, b in students:
    c_values.append(a - b)

# Sort by c_i in descending order
sorted_c = sorted(c_values, reverse=True)

# Calculate the variable part: sum of c_i * position
variable_sum = 0
for i, c in enumerate(sorted_c):
    position = i + 1  # 1-indexed position
    variable_sum += c * position

# Calculate the constant part: sum of (b_i * n - a_i) for all students
constant_sum = 0
for a, b in students:
    constant_sum += b * n - a

# Total dissatisfaction
total_dissatisfaction = variable_sum + constant_sum
print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

Wait, I need to preserve the original (a,b) pairs for the constant sum, and I'm using only the c_values for sorting. I also need to ensure I'm calculating the constant sum correctly.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

During a break in the buffet of the scientific lyceum of the Kingdom of Kremland, there was formed a queue of $n$ high school students numbered from $1$ to $n$. Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Dissatisfaction of the person $i$ equals the product of $a_i$ by the number of people standing to the left of his position, add the product $b_i$ by the number of people standing to the right of his position. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 2
2 3
6 1

Output
12
Input
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

Output
25
Input
10
5 10
12 4
31 45
20 55
30 17
29 30
41 32
7 1
5 5
3 15

Output
1423


-----Note-----

In the first example it is optimal to put people in this order: ($3, 1, 2$). The first person is in the position of $2$, then his dissatisfaction will be equal to $4 \cdot 1+2 \cdot 1=6$. The second person is in the position of $3$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $2 \cdot 2+3 \cdot 0=4$. The third person is in the position of $1$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $6 \cdot 0+1 \cdot 2=2$. The total dissatisfaction will be $12$.

In the second example, you need to put people in this order: ($3, 2, 4, 1$). ## Problem Understanding

We have n students, each with characteristics a_i and b_i. When student i is at position j, their dissatisfaction is:
- a_i × (j-1) + b_i × (n-j)

We want to minimize the total dissatisfaction across all students.

## Key Insight

Let's rewrite the dissatisfaction formula:
- a_i × (j-1) + b_i × (n-j) = a_i × j - a_i + b_i × n - b_i × j
- = (a_i - b_i) × j + (b_i × n - a_i)

This means for each student i, their dissatisfaction depends on their position j through the term (a_i - b_i) × j.

## Optimization Strategy

Since we want to minimize total dissatisfaction, and each student's contribution is (a_i - b_i) × j + constant, we need to assign students to positions optimally.

The key insight is that students with a higher (a_i - b_i) value should go to the right (higher j), while students with a lower (a_i - b_i) value should go to the left (lower j).

For student i at position j:
- Contribution = (a_i - b_i) × j + (b_i × n - a_i)

To minimize this, we want to:
- If (a_i - b_i) > 0: we want smaller j (left positions)
- If (a_i - b_i) < 0: we want larger j (right positions)

2. Sort students by d_i in ascending order
3. Assign the students to positions 1 to n in this sorted order
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction

## Final Algorithm

1. For each student, calculate d_i = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by d_i in descending order
3. Assign students to positions 1 to n in this order
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction = Σ[(a_i - b_i) × j_i + (b_i × n - a_i)]

Since the second part is constant, we can compute it once and add it to the first part.

Actually, let me compute it properly:

For each student i at position j:
- contribution = (a_i - b_i) × j + (b_i × n - a_i)

Total = Σ[(a_i - b_i) × j_i] + Σ(b_i × n - a_i)

The second sum is independent of arrangement: Σ(b_i × n - a_i) = n × Σb_i - Σa_i

The first sum depends on the ordering.

So we can:
1. Calculate base = n × Σb_i - Σa_i
2. Sort students by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
3. For i from 0 to n-1, position is i+1
4. Sum up (a_i - b_i) × (i+1) for all students in sorted order
5. Total = base + this sum

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
total_b = 0
total_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))
    total_b += b
    total_a += a

# Base dissatisfaction term (independent of ordering)
base = n * total_b - total_a

# Create list of (a - b) values and sort in descending order
differences = []
for a, b in students:
    differences.append(a - b)

# Sort differences in descending order
differences.sort(reverse=True)

# Calculate sum of (a_i - b_i) * position
pos_value_sum = 0
for i, diff in enumerate(differences):
    pos_value_sum += diff * (i + 1)

total_dissatisfaction = base + pos_value_sum
print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`. When student `i` is at position `j` (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is:
- `a_i * (j-1)` + `b_i * (n-j)`

Total dissatisfaction is the sum of individual dissatisfactions across all students.

We want to minimize the total dissatisfaction by optimally rearranging students.

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j) = a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- `= (a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for student `i` at position `j`, dissatisfaction = `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

When we sum over all students, the total dissatisfaction becomes:
- Σ[(a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i)] for all students i at position j_i

This can be broken down as:
- Σ(a_i - b_i) * j_i + Σ(b_i * n - a_i)

The second sum is constant regardless of arrangement (since it doesn't depend on positions):
- Σ(b_i * n - a_i) = n * Σ(b_i) - Σ(a_i)

So we only need to minimize the first sum: Σ(a_i - b_i) * j_i

To minimize this sum, we should assign students with small (a_i - b_i) values to large positions (high j_i), and students with large (a_i - b_i) values to small positions.

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we want to minimize Σ(a_i - b_i) * j_i, we want:
- If (a_i - b_i) is negative, we want to place this student at a large j_i (positive contribution)
- If (a_i - b_i) is positive, we want to place this student at a small j_i (negative contribution)

Actually, let me think about this more systematically.

The coefficient of j_i is (a_i - b_i). To minimize the sum, we should:
- For students with (a_i - b_i) > 0, we want them to have small j_i (small position)
- For students with (a_i - b_i) < 0, we want them to have large j_i (large position)

This means we should sort students by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order, and place them from position 1 to n.

## Algorithm

1. For each student, calculate the value (a_i - b_i)
2. Sort students by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order
3. Assign the student with the smallest (a_i - b_i) to position 1, next to position 2, etc.
4. Calculate the total dissatisfaction

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b, i))  # (a, b, original_index)

# Calculate a_i - b_i for each student
# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1])

total_dissatisfaction = 0

# Calculate total dissatisfaction
for pos in range(n):
    a, b, orig_idx = students[pos]
    # Student at position (pos + 1) - 1 (0-indexed position)
    current_pos = pos + 1
    dissatisfaction = a * (current_pos - 1) + b * (n - current_pos)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

= (a_i - b_i)*j + (b_i*n - a_i)

Since j_i increases, we want to assign negative (a_i - b_i) to large j_i, and positive (a_i - b_i) to small j_i.

If (a_i - b_i) is positive, we want j_i to be small (since positive times small = small positive).

- Assign smallest (a_i - b_i) to largest position (j_i)
- Assign largest (a_i - b_i) to smallest position (j_i)

- Positive (a_i - b_i): want small j_i

So the correct way is to sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order, and then assign to descending positions? 

No, simpler: just sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order, and assign to positions 1, 2, ..., n.

When (a_i - b_i) is large (positive), we put them at small j_i.
When (a_i - b_i) is small (negative), we put them at large j_i.

This works.

```python
n = int(input())
students = []

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0

for pos in range(n):
    a, b = students[pos]
    j = pos + 1  # 1-indexed position
    dissatisfaction = a * (j - 1) + b * (n - j)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

During a break in the buffet of the scientific lyceum of the Kingdom of Kremland, there was formed a queue of $n$ high school students numbered from $1$ to $n$. Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Dissatisfaction of the person $i$ equals the product of $a_i$ by the number of people standing to the left of his position, add the product $b_i$ by the number of people standing to the right of his position. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 2
2 3
6 1

Output
12
Input
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

Output
25
Input
10
5 10
12 4
31 45
20 55
30 17
29 30
41 32
7 1
5 5
3 15

Output
1423


-----Note-----

In the first example it is optimal to put people in this order: ($3, 1, 2$). The first person is in the position of $2$, then his dissatisfaction will be equal to $4 \cdot 1+2 \cdot 1=6$. The second person is in the position of $3$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $2 \cdot 2+3 \cdot 0=4$. The third person is in the position of $1$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $6 \cdot 0+1 \cdot 2=2$. The total dissatisfaction will be $12$.

In the second example, you need to put people in this order: ($3, 2, 4, 1$). The total dissatisfaction will be $25$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). ## Problem Understanding

We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`. When student `i` is at position `j` (1-indexed), their dissatisfaction is:
- `a_i * (j-1)` + `b_i * (n-j)`

Total dissatisfaction is the sum over all students.

## Key Insight

Let me analyze the dissatisfaction formula:
- For student `i` at position `j`: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
- This can be rewritten as: `a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- Which simplifies to: `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So for each student, their contribution to total dissatisfaction depends on their position `j` through the coefficient `(a_i - b_i)`.

## Strategy

Since we want to minimize total dissatisfaction, we should:
- Students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` should go to positions with higher indices (since they get multiplied by a larger positive coefficient)
- Students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` should go to positions with lower indices (since they get multiplied by a smaller positive coefficient)

So we should sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order. Students with smaller `(a_i - b_i)` should go to the left, students with larger `(a_i - b_i)` should go to the right.

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order. Students with very negative `(a_i - b_i)` go to the right, students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` go to the left.

- Students with `(a_i - b_i)` very positive: they want to go left (smaller `j`)

So we should sort by `(a_i - b_i)` in increasing order. The smallest `(a_i - b_i)` go to position 1, the largest go to position n.

This can be rewritten as:

- If w_i > 0, we want to minimize position j (to make w_i * j as small as possible)

But to minimize the sum, we should pair students with large negative w_i with large j, and students with positive w_i with small j.

## Algorithm

1. For each student i, compute w_i = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by w_i in ascending order
3. Assign positions in descending order: the student with smallest w_i gets position n, the next gets n-1, etc.
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction = sum_i (w_i * position_i) + sum_i (b_i * n - a_i)
   - where sum_i (b_i * n - a_i) is independent of arrangement

Actually, we can compute the sum directly without explicitly assigning positions.

Since we sort by w_i ascending and assign descending positions:
- For student k (k from 0 to n-1 in sorted order), position = n - k
- Contribution to sum = w_k * (n - k)

So total = sum_{k=0}^{n-1} (a_k - b_k) * (n - k) + sum_i (b_i * n - a_i)

Wait, no: the sum of (a_i-b_i)*position_i is computed with the assignment.

The constant sum is sum_i (b_i * n - a_i) = n * sum(b_i) - sum(a_i)

For the assignment sum, we have sorted array of w values and positions n, n-1, ..., 1.

So total dissatisfaction = sum_{k=0}^{n-1} w_k * (n - k) + n * sum(b_i) - sum(a_i)

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
sum_b = 0
sum_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a - b, a, b))  # (w, a, b)
    sum_b += b
    sum_a += a

# Sort by w in ascending order
students.sort()

# Calculate sum of w_k * (n-k) for k in 0 to n-1
assignment_sum = 0
for k in range(n):
    w, a, b = students[k]
    position = n - k  # 1-indexed position
    assignment_sum += w * position

constant_sum = n * sum_b - sum_a

total_dissatisfaction = assignment_sum + constant_sum

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

In the example:
- k=0: w=-1, position=3 → -3
- k=1: w=2, position=2 → 4  
- k=2: w=5, position=1 → 5
Sum = 6

constant_sum = 3*(2+3+1) - (4+2+6) = 18 - 12 = 6
Total = 12 ✓

Perfect!

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
sum_b = 0
sum_a = 0

for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a - b, a, b))
    sum_b += b
    sum_a += a

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in ascending order
students.sort()

# Calculate the sum of (a_i - b_i) * position_i where position_i is assigned in descending order
assignment_sum = 0
for k in range(n):
    w, a, b = students[k]
    position = n - k  # 1-indexed position, from n down to 1
    assignment_sum += w * position

# The constant term: sum of (b_i * n - a_i) for all i
constant_sum = n * sum_b - sum_a

total_dissatisfaction = assignment_sum + constant_sum
print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

Initially, each student $i$ is on position $i$. Each student $i$ is characterized by two numbers — $a_i$ and $b_i$. Dissatisfaction of the person $i$ equals the product of $a_i$ by the number of people standing to the left of his position, add the product $b_i$ by the number of people standing to the right of his position. Formally, the dissatisfaction of the student $i$, which is on the position $j$, equals $a_i \cdot (j-1) + b_i \cdot (n-j)$.

The director entrusted Stas with the task: rearrange the people in the queue so that minimize the total dissatisfaction.

Although Stas is able to solve such problems, this was not given to him. He turned for help to you.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $n$ ($1 \leq n \leq 10^5$) — the number of people in the queue.

Each of the following $n$ lines contains two integers $a_i$ and $b_i$ ($1 \leq a_i, b_i \leq 10^8$) — the characteristic of the student $i$, initially on the position $i$.


-----Output-----

Output one integer — minimum total dissatisfaction which can be achieved by rearranging people in the queue.


-----Examples-----
Input
3
4 2
2 3
6 1

Output
12
Input
4
2 4
3 3
7 1
2 3

Output
25
Input
10
5 10
12 4
31 45
20 55
30 17
29 30
41 32
7 1
5 5
3 15

Output
1423


-----Note-----

In the first example it is optimal to put people in this order: ($3, 1, 2$). The first person is in the position of $2$, then his dissatisfaction will be equal to $4 \cdot 1+2 \cdot 1=6$. The second person is in the position of $3$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $2 \cdot 2+3 \cdot 0=4$. The third person is in the position of $1$, his dissatisfaction will be equal to $6 \cdot 0+1 \cdot 2=2$. The total dissatisfaction will be $12$.

In the second example, you need to put people in this order: ($3, 2, 4, 1$). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have `n` students, each with characteristics `a_i` and `b_i`
2. Each student's dissatisfaction when at position `j` is: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
3. We need to minimize the total dissatisfaction of all students when rearranged optimally

## Mathematical Analysis

Let's expand the dissatisfaction formula:
- For student `i` at position `j`: `a_i * (j-1) + b_i * (n-j)`
- This equals: `a_i * j - a_i + b_i * n - b_i * j`
- This equals: `(a_i - b_i) * j + (b_i * n - a_i)`

So the total dissatisfaction is:
- Sum over all students: `(a_i - b_i) * j_i + (b_i * n - a_i)`
- Where `j_i` is the position of student `i`

We can separate this into:
- Sum of `(a_i - b_i) * j_i` + Sum of `(b_i * n - a_i)`

The second sum is constant regardless of arrangement (it's just sum over all students of `b_i * n - a_i`).

So we only need to minimize the first sum: `sum((a_i - b_i) * j_i)`.

This is a classic assignment problem where we want to assign positions to students to minimize weighted sum.

## Key Insight

To minimize `sum((a_i - b_i) * j_i)`, we should:
- Sort students by the value `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order
- Assign the smallest `(a_i - b_i)` to the smallest position (1), next smallest to position 2, etc.

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we want to minimize the weighted sum with coefficients `(a_i - b_i)`, we should:
- Assign students with negative `(a_i - b_i)` to the left (smaller positions) because they have negative weights
- Assign students with positive `(a_i - b_i)` to the right (larger positions) because their contribution to the sum will be smaller

Actually, the standard greedy approach for minimizing weighted sum is to sort by the coefficient in ascending order and assign to positions in increasing order.

So we sort students by `(a_i - b_i)` in ascending order, then assign position 1, 2, ..., n respectively.

## Algorithm

1. For each student i, calculate c_i = a_i - b_i
2. Sort students by c_i in descending order
3. Assign position 1 to the student with highest (a_i - b_i), position 2 to next, etc.
4. Calculate total dissatisfaction using the formula

Total dissatisfaction = sum over i of [a_i * (j_i - 1) + b_i * (n - j_i)]

We can compute this directly or use the derived formula.

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
students = []
for i in range(n):
    a, b = map(int, input().split())
    students.append((a, b))

# Sort by (a_i - b_i) in descending order
students.sort(key=lambda x: x[0] - x[1], reverse=True)

total_dissatisfaction = 0
for i in range(n):
    a, b = students[i]
    position = i + 1
    dissatisfaction = a * (position - 1) + b * (n - position)
    total_dissatisfaction += dissatisfaction

print(total_dissatisfaction)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y is divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x mod 5 + y mod 5) mod 5 = 0.

This means:
- If x mod 5 = 0, then y mod 5 must be 0
- If x mod 5 = 1, then y mod 5 must be 4  
- If x mod 5 = 2, then y mod 5 must be 3
- If x mod 5 = 3, then y mod 5 must be 2
- If x mod 5 = 4, then y mod 5 must be 1

## Approach

1. Count how many numbers in range [1, n] have each remainder when divided by 5
2. Count how many numbers in range [1, m] have each remainder when divided by 5
3. For each valid pair of remainders (r1, r2) where (r1 + r2) mod 5 = 0, multiply the counts and add to result

## Step-by-step solution

Let's count remainders for numbers 1 to n:
- Numbers with remainder 0: floor(n/5)
- Numbers with remainder 1: floor((n-1)/5) + 1 if n % 5 >= 1, else 0
- Actually, simpler: remainder r appears floor((n-r)/5) + 1 times for r in [1,4], and floor(n/5) times for r=0

For any range [1, k]:
- Remainder 0: k // 5
- Remainder 1: (k + 4) // 5  
- Remainder 2: (k + 3) // 5
- Remainder 3: (k + 2) // 5
- Remainder 4: (k + 1) // 5

Wait, let me verify: numbers with remainder r in [1,k] = floor((k - r) / 5) + 1 = (k - r + 4) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 4) // 5 + 1

Actually, simpler: 
- Count of numbers with remainder r mod 5 in [1,k] = (k - r) // 5 + 1 (if r <= k, else 0)

But easier: 
- remainder 0: k // 5
- remainder 1: (k + 4) // 5
- remainder 2: (k + 3) // 5  
- remainder 3: (k + 2) // 5
- remainder 4: (k + 1) // 5

Let me verify with k=6:
- remainder 0: 6//5 = 1 (number: 5)
- remainder 1: (6+4)//5 = 10//5 = 2 (numbers: 1, 6)
- remainder 2: (6+3)//5 = 9//5 = 1 (number: 2)
- remainder 3: (6+2)//5 = 8//5 = 1 (number: 3)
- remainder 4: (6+1)//5 = 7//5 = 1 (number: 4)

Perfect! This gives us the right counts.

## Algorithm

1. Calculate counts for remainders 0,1,2,3,4 in range [1,n]
2. Calculate counts for remainders 0,1,2,3,4 in range [1,m]
3. For each pair (r1, r2) where (r1 + r2) % 5 == 0, add count_n[r1] * count_m[r2]

The valid pairs are:
- (0,0)
- (1,4)
- (2,3)
- (3,2)
- (4,1)

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
count_m = [0] * 5

# For range [1, n]
count_n[0] = n // 5
count_n[1] = (n + 4) // 5  # This is equivalent to (n - 1) // 5 + 1
count_n[2] = (n + 3) // 5
count_n[3] = (n + 2) // 5
count_n[4] = (n + 1) // 5

# For range [1, m]
count_m[0] = m // 5
count_m[1] = (m + 4) // 5
count_m[2] = (m + 3) // 5
count_m[3] = (m + 2) // 5
count_m[4] = (m + 1) // 5

# Count pairs where (x + y) mod 5 = 0
result = 0

# (0,0): both have remainder 0
result += count_n[0] * count_m[0]

# (1,4): x mod 5 = 1, y mod 5 = 4
result += count_n[1] * count_m[4]

# (2,3): x mod 5 = 2, y mod 5 = 3
result += count_n[2] * count_m[3]

# (3,2): x mod 5 = 3, y mod 5 = 2
result += count_n[3] * count_m[2]

# (4,1): x mod 5 = 4, y mod 5 = 1
result += count_n[4] * count_m[1]

print(result)
```

Wait, let me double-check the remainder counting formula.

For remainder r in [1,k]:
- The numbers are: r, r+5, r+10, ... 
- This forms an arithmetic sequence with first term r, common difference 5
- Last term ≤ k, so r + 5t ≤ k, where t ≥ 0
- t ≤ (k - r) / 5
- Number of terms = floor((k - r) / 5) + 1

So count of numbers with remainder r in [1,k] = (k - r) // 5 + 1 (if r ≤ k, else 0)

For r=0: numbers are 5, 10, 15, ... so count = k // 5

So general formula:
- remainder 0: k // 5
- remainder r (1 ≤ r ≤ 4): (k - r) // 5 + 1

But (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5 = (k + 5 - r) // 5

Since (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5.

Let me verify with k=6, r=1: (6-1)//5 + 1 = 5//5 + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2 ✓
(6+5-1)//5 = 10//5 = 2 ✓

Actually, (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5 only when (k-r) is not divisible by 5? No, it's always true.

(k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5

So formula is:
- remainder 0: k // 5
- remainder r: (k - r) // 5 + 1 = (k - r + 5) // 5

But (k - r + 5) // 5 = (k + 5 - r) // 5

For r=1: (k + 4) // 5
For r=2: (k + 3) // 5
For r=3: (k + 2) // 5  
For r=4: (k + 1) // 5

Yes! This matches what I had earlier.

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
count_n[0] = n // 5
count_n[1] = (n + 4) // 5
count_n[2] = (n + 3) // 5
count_n[3] = (n + 2) // 5
count_n[4] = (n + 1) // 5

# Count remainders for m
count_m = [0] * 5
count_m[0] = m // 5
count_m[1] = (m + 4) // 5
count_m[2] = (m + 3) // 5
count_m[3] = (m + 2) // 5
count_m[4] = (m + 1) // 5

# Count pairs where (x + y) mod 5 = 0
result = 0

# (0,0): both have remainder 0
result += count_n[0] * count_m[0]

# (1,4): x mod 5 = 1, y mod 5 = 4
result += count_n[1] * count_m[4]

# (2,3): x mod 5 = 2, y mod 5 = 3
result += count_n[2] * count_m[3]

# (3,2): x mod 5 = 3, y mod 5 = 2
result += count_n[3] * count_m[2]

# (4,1): x mod 5 = 4, y mod 5 = 1
result += count_n[4] * count_m[1]

print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m  
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y should be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) ≡ 0 (mod 5), which means:
- x ≡ -y (mod 5)
- Since -y (mod 5) is equivalent to (5 - y%5) % 5, we need x ≡ (5 - y%5) % 5 (mod 5)

Alternatively, we can think about the remainders when x and y are divided by 5.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count remainders**: For each number from 1 to n, we can count how many give remainder r when divided by 5 (for r = 0, 1, 2, 3, 4).
2. **Count remainders**: Similarly for numbers from 1 to m.
3. **Pair remainders**: We need pairs where (r1 + r2) ≡ 0 (mod 5).

The valid pairs of remainders are:
- (0, 0): 0 + 0 = 0 (divisible by 5)
- (1, 4): 1 + 4 = 5 (divisible by 5)
- (2, 3): 2 + 3 = 5 (divisible by 5)
- (3, 2): 3 + 2 = 5 (divisible by 5)
- (4, 1): 4 + 1 = 5 (divisible by 5)

Note: (1,4) and (4,1) are different pairs.

## Algorithm

1. Count remainders for numbers 1 to n:
   - count_n[0] = n // 5
   - count_n[1] = (n - 1 + 5) // 5
   - count_n[2] = (n - 2 + 5) // 5
   - count_n[3] = (n - 3 + 5) // 5
   - count_n[4] = (n - 4 + 5) // 5

2. Count remainders for numbers 1 to m:
   - count_m[0] = m // 5
   - count_m[1] = (m - 1 + 5) // 5
   - count_m[2] = (m - 2 + 5) // 5
   - count_m[3] = (m - 3 + 5) // 5
   - count_m[4] = (m - 4 + 5) // 5

3. Pair remainders:
   - (0,0): count_n[0] * count_m[0]
   - (1,4): count_n[1] * count_m[4]
   - (2,3): count_n[2] * count_m[3]
   - (3,2): count_n[3] * count_m[2]
   - (4,1): count_n[4] * count_m[1]

Total pairs = sum of these.

## Verification with sample

n=6, m=12

count_n[0] = 6//5 = 1
count_n[1] = (6-1+5)//5 = 10//5 = 2
count_n[2] = (6-2+5)//5 = 9//5 = 1
count_n[3] = (6-3+5)//5 = 8//5 = 1
count_n[4] = (6-4+5)//5 = 7//5 = 1

count_m[0] = 12//5 = 2
count_m[1] = (12-1+5)//5 = 16//5 = 3
count_m[2] = (12-2+5)//5 = 15//5 = 3
count_m[3] = (12-3+5)//5 = 14//5 = 2
count_m[4] = (12-4+5)//5 = 13//5 = 2

Pairs:
- (0,0): 1*2 = 2
- (1,4): 2*2 = 4
- (2,3): 1*2 = 2
- (3,2): 1*3 = 3
- (4,1): 1*3 = 3

Total = 2+4+2+3+3 = 14 ✓

Perfect!

## Code Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for n
count_n = [0] * 5
count_n[0] = n // 5
count_n[1] = (n - 1 + 5) // 5
count_n[2] = (n - 2 + 5) // 5
count_n[3] = (n - 3 + 5) // 5
count_n[4] = (n - 4 + 5) // 5

# Count remainders for m
count_m = [0] * 5
count_m[0] = m // 5
count_m[1] = (m - 1 + 5) // 5
count_m[2] = (m - 2 + 5) // 5
count_m[3] = (m - 3 + 5) // 5
count_m[4] = (m - 4 + 5) // 5

# Pair remainders
total = count_n[0] * count_m[0]  # (0,0)
total += count_n[1] * count_m[4]  # (1,4)
total += count_n[2] * count_m[3]  # (2,3)
total += count_n[3] * count_m[2]  # (3,2)
total += count_n[4] * count_m[1]  # (4,1)

print(total)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

We need to count pairs (x, y) where:
- 1 ≤ x ≤ n
- 1 ≤ y ≤ m
- (x + y) mod 5 = 0

This means x + y must be divisible by 5.

## Key Insight

For (x + y) to be divisible by 5, we need (x + y) % 5 = 0.
This means y % 5 = (5 - x % 5) % 5.

So for each possible remainder of x when divided by 5, we need to find how many y values have the complementary remainder.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Count remainders of x values (1 to n)**
   - For each remainder r (0, 1, 2, 3, 4), count how many numbers in [1, n] have remainder r when divided by 5.

2. **Count remainders of y values (1 to m)**
   - Similarly, for each remainder s (0, 1, 2, 3, 4), count how many numbers in [1, m] have remainder s when divided by 5.

3. **Pair complementary remainders**
   - For each remainder r in x, we need y with remainder (5 - r) % 5
   - So pairs are formed between:
     - x with remainder 0 and y with remainder 0
     - x with remainder 1 and y with remainder 4
     - x with remainder 2 and y with remainder 3
     - x with remainder 3 and y with remainder 2
     - x with remainder 4 and y with remainder 1

4. **Sum all valid pairs**

## Efficiency

Instead of counting remainders for each individual number, we can calculate the counts directly.

For numbers from 1 to k:
- Count of numbers with remainder 0: k // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 1: (k + 4) // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 2: (k + 3) // 5  
- Count of numbers with remainder 3: (k + 2) // 5
- Count of numbers with remainder 4: (k + 1) // 5

## Implementation

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count remainders for numbers 1 to n
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    count_x[r] = (n - r + 4) // 5 if r > 0 else n // 5
    
# Actually, simpler approach: for each remainder, count how many numbers <= n have that remainder
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    # Numbers with remainder r: r, r+5, r+10, ... <= n
    # First number is r (if r >= 1) or 5 (if r = 0)
    # General formula: floor((n - r) / 5) + 1 if r <= n, else 0
    if r == 0:
        count_x[r] = n // 5
    else:
        if r <= n:
            count_x[r] = (n - r) // 5 + 1
        else:
            count_x[r] = 0

# Better approach: use the fact that in range [1, n], remainder r appears floor((n-r)/5) + 1 times when r <= n
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    if r == 0:
        count_x[r] = n // 5
    else:
        count_x[r] = (n - r + 4) // 5  # This is equivalent to floor((n-r)/5) + 1

# Let me recalculate more simply:
def count_remainder(n, r):
    if r == 0:
        return n // 5
    else:
        # Numbers with remainder r: r, r+5, r+10, ... <= n
        # This is floor((n - r) / 5) + 1 if r <= n, else 0
        if r > n:
            return 0
        return (n - r) // 5 + 1

count_x = [count_remainder(n, r) for r in range(5)]
count_y = [count_remainder(m, r) for r in range(5)]

# For sum divisible by 5: x%5 + y%5 = 0 (mod 5)
# So we need pairs where remainder_x + remainder_y ≡ 0 (mod 5)
total_pairs = 0
for i in range(5):
    j = (5 - i) % 5
    total_pairs += count_x[i] * count_y[j]

print(total_pairs)
```

Wait, let me verify this with a simpler and more direct approach:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count frequencies of remainders for first column (1 to n)
count_x = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    # In numbers 1 to n, how many have remainder r when divided by 5?
    # If r = 0: numbers 5, 10, 15, ... -> count = n // 5
    # If r > 0: numbers r, r+5, r+10, ... <= n
    if r == 0:
        count_x[r] = n // 5
    else:
        # First number is r, then r+5, r+10, ...
        # Last number <= n is r + 5*k <= n, so k <= (n-r)/5
        # Count = floor((n-r)/5) + 1
        count_x[r] = (n - r) // 5 + 1

# Count frequencies of remainders for second column (1 to m)
count_y = [0] * 5
for r in range(5):
    if r == 0:
        count_y[r] = m // 5
    else:
        count_y[r] = (m - r) // 5 + 1

# Count pairs where (x + y) % 5 == 0
# This happens when (x % 5 + y % 5) % 5 == 0
# So we pair remainders: 0+0, 1+4, 2+3, 3+2, 4+1
result = 0
for i in range(5):
    j = (5 - i) % 5
    result += count_x[i] * count_y[j]

print(result)
```

Actually, let me simplify this even more and fix the edge cases:

```python
n, m = map(int, input().split())

# Count how many numbers in [1,n] have remainder r when divided by 5
def count_remainder(k, r):
    if r == 0:
        return k // 5
    else:
        # r, r+5, r+10, ... <= k
        # The sequence starts at r and increases by 5
        # If r > k, no such numbers
        if r > k:
            return 0
        # Number of terms = ((k - r) // 5) + 1
        return (k - r) // 5 + 1

# Count remainders for x in [1,n]
count_x = [count_remainder(n, r) for r in range(5)]

# Count remainders for y in [1,m]
count_y = [count_remainder(m, r) for r in range(5)]

# Pairs where (x+y) % 5 == 0
# i + j ≡ 0 (mod 5) where i = x%5, j = y%5
result = 0
for i in range(5):
    j = (5 - i) % 5
    result += count_x[i] * count_y[j]

print(result)
```
